하나증권•17

Weekly Issue: Section 232, 반도체까지 엮인 Upstream 구축이 핵심(2026년 8/10일 발간자료 발췌) 
 Section 232, 반도체까지 엮인 Upstream 구축이 핵심 
- Fack Check 
1) 12/4 일 이후 폴리실리콘과 그 파생상품에 대해 MIP(Minimum Import Price; 최소수입가격) 설정. 폴리실리콘 21$/kg, 
잉곳/웨이퍼 100$/kg, 셀 0.22$/W, 모듈 0.38$/W. 향후 MIP 조정 가능. 자국 내 공급망에 대한 수익성 근거 마련 
2) 별도 15% 관세는 폴리실리콘을 제외한 잉곳/웨이퍼/셀/모듈에 적용. Downstream 원가 부담 경감 목적 
3) 2029년 1/20일까지 생산설비 증설 및 확장 시(모듈 제외) 신규 투자금에 상응하는 생산장비와 관련 제품을 Section 232 관세 
없이 수입 가능. Upstream 생산설비 확대 인센티브 제공 목적 
4) 8/6일 이전 고정계약(Fixed Terms)은 MIP 예외 적용. 기존 계약의 안정성 보호 목적 
5) 12/4일 이전 사재기(Stockfiling) 적발 시, 수입 제한 조치 시행. 정상적인 가격 메커니즘 작동 목적 
- 주요 사항 해석 
1) 태양광/반도체 Upstream 공급망 구축을 통한 국가 안보가 핵심: 중국의 저가공세로 미국 태양광용 폴리실리콘 등 Upstream 
공급망이 붕괴하면서, 반도체용 폴리실리콘/웨이퍼 공급망 지속 가능성에도 부정적 영향 불가피. 이는 AI/반도체 산업 및 국가 
안보에 중장기적 위협. 따라서, 이번 정책은 미국 내 강력한 태양광/반도체 Upstream 공급망 구축을 위한 밑그림. 이미 
공급과잉인 모듈은 제조 인센티브 혜택에서 제외된다는 점이 이를 증명. 특히, 실제, 미국 내 폴리실리콘 공장은 2곳에 불과. 
Hemlock Semiconductor(Corning/Shin -Etsu JV) 은 미시간에, Wacker Chemie 는 테네시 공장 보유. 두 업체는 반도체용 
폴리실리콘에서도 독보적 위치. 2025년 미국 상무부는 CHIPS Act 지원금으로 Hemlock의 반도체용 초고순도 폴리실리콘 전용 
공장 프로젝트를 지원. Wacker는 글로벌 No.1 반도체용 폴리실리콘 업체로 CEO는 최근 태양광보다 반도체용 폴리실리콘에 
집중할 계획을 밝힌 바 있어    
2) 폴리실리콘 MIP 21$/kg, Hemlock/Wacker 를 보호하고 증설을 유도하기 위한 가격: 비중국산 폴리실리콘 업체 중 유일한 
미국업체인 Hemlock의 변동비는 20$/kg, Wacker는 16$/kg로 추정. 결국, MIP 21$/kg은 미국 내 생산업체를 보호하고 추가적인 
증설을 유도하기 위한 최소한의 가격대로 판단한 것으로 보임. 참고로, 21$/kg은 현 가격 16$/kg 대비 31% 상승 여력  
3) 폴리실리콘 추가 관세 15%가 없어도 비중국산 경쟁력 충분: 중국산 폴리실리콘에는 이미 2025년 1월 초부터 Section 301 
관세 50%가 적용되며, UFLPA를 통한 수입금지 제도도 마련. MIP 21$/kg에 50% 관세 적용 시 중국산 폴리실리콘은 31.5$/kg로 
경쟁력 상실 불가피. 반면, 말레이 OCI홀딩스, 독일 Wacker 등 비중국산 폴리실리콘 MIP는 21$/kg로 이미 경쟁력 충분. 즉, 
폴리실리콘은 추가 관세 15% 없이도 중국산에 대한 장벽이 충분하다고 판단한 듯 
4) 왜 잉곳/웨이퍼/셀/모듈에만 추가 관세 15%가 붙나: 미국은 모듈 대비 잉곳/웨이퍼/셀 Capa가 현저히 부족해 대부분 수입에 
의존. 대규모 증설 불가피. 즉, MIP에 관세를 얹어 해당 제품 증설의 유인을 더욱 높이겠다는 의도. 반면, 폴리실리콘에 추가 관세 
15%를 부과하면 밸류체인 전반에 원가 부담 가중. 따라서, 이미 중국산에 대한 장벽이 충분한 폴리실리콘에만 추가 관세 15%가 
붙지 않은 것으로 판단 
5) 잉곳/웨이퍼 MIP는 매우 높아 중요한 변수: 잉곳/웨이퍼 MIP 100$/kg은 OCI홀딩스 기준 10~12cent/W로 중국산 2cent/W, 
미국향 3~4cent/W 대비 3~4배 이상 높아. Wells Fargo 는 +8cent/W의 상승 효과로 분석. 여기에 추가 관세 15%까지 붙어. 
웨이퍼의 증설 유인 높이기 위한 강력한 조치  
6) 태양광 프로젝트 수익성 악화를 상쇄할 내용은 부족: 이번 정책은 태양광/반도체 공급망을 구축하기 위해 태양광 프로젝트 
수익성 악화라는 리스크를 감내하겠다는 의도. 결국, 중장기적으로 밸류체인의 가격이 급등할 경우 및 수요 감소하면서 가격 
상승의 상한이 제한될 수 있는 요소  
- 관련 업체별 영향: OCI홀딩스, 이익 레버리지 효과 극대화 예상 
1) OCI홀딩스: 웨이퍼가 서프라이즈 
- 미국 태양광/반도체용 폴리실리콘 공급망 구축과 관련해 한화솔루션/스페이스X/테슬라 등 미국 내 공급망을 구축한 업체들을 
고객사로 보유할 가능성이 높음. Musk의 미국 내 폴리실리콘 공급망 구축과 관련하여 협업 등을 기대할 수 있는 점도 기대. 
그룹차원에서 반도체용 폴리실리콘 뿐 아니라, 반도체 소재까지 확장하고 있다는 점도 긍정적

(이어서 계속)

### Page 18

에너지/화학 Weekly Monitor 2026.10.12

하나증권•18 
(이어서 계속) 
- 현재 폴리실리콘 Capa 3.5만톤 기준 현재 가격에서 MIP까지 +5$/kg 상승 시, 매출액 연간 약 2.5천억원 개선(내부 소요분 감안 
시 이보다 낮아짐). 원가변동 크지 않으므로 대부분 이익 귀속 가능    
- 베트남 NST 웨이퍼 2.7GW는 말레이 폴리로 생산하는 Non-PFE 공급망으로 미국향 수출에 유리. 미국 내 셀 업체는 웨이퍼 
공급 부족 영향으로 높은 MIP가 적용된 수입산 웨이퍼에 높은 프리미엄 지급 가능성 상존. MIP까지 약 +7~8cent/W 상승 가정 
시 2.7~3천억원 매출액 개선 효과. 내부 원가 상승은 없으므로, 이에 상응하는 높은 이익 레버리지 발생 가능 
- OCI홀딩스는 폴리실리콘은 물론 웨이퍼 증설을 통한 미국 판매 확대가 더 높은 이익 레버리지를 발생시킬 가능성. 이는 웨이퍼 
자회사 베트남 NST 가치 부각 요인. 이미 회사는 2028~29년 물량이 LTA 체결로 완판되었음을 언급. 현재 2.7GW에서 2027년 
3.75GW, 2028년 7.5GW, 2029년 11.5GW로 확장할 계획    
- 리스크: 8/6일 이전 고정계약(Fixed Term)이 예외로 인정 시, 포뮬러에 따라 가격 변동되어 MIP 가격 이하에서 거래될 수 있음. 
이는 결국, 앞서 살펴본 이론적 개선 효과가 상쇄되는 요인 
2) 한화솔루션: 수직계열화로 더욱 강력한 경쟁력 갖춰  
- 미국 모듈 평균 판가 30cent/W에서 MIP 38cent/W 적용 시, 최소 가격 상승률 +27% 수준. 추가 15% 관세 부과 시 가격 
상승률은 더욱 커질 것. 9GW 출하량 가이던스 감안 시, +8ccnt/W로 매출액 약 1조원 가량 증가 효과 발생. 특히, 카터스빌은 
잉곳/웨이퍼/셀/모듈 수직계열화로 밸류체인 가격 상승과 AMPC 추가 확대 효과를 모두 누릴 수 있음. 다만, 카터스빌에서의 
폴리실리콘 원가 상승 및 달튼에서의 셀 원가 상승분을 감안하면 실질 이익 증가분은 이에 미치지 못할 듯 
- 경쟁사는 원료 외부 구매로 원가가 상승하나 카터스빌은 자체 공급망 구축으로 상대적 원가 우위를 누릴 수 있어 
- 리스크/기회:  달튼 공장은 셀을 외부구매 하므로 모듈 판가 상승분을 셀 원가 상승분이 대부분 상쇄할 가능성 존재. 다만, 
2029년 1/20일까지 추가 투자 확대 시, 상응하는 금액만큼 Section232 관세 없이 수입 가능. 이후 셀 공장 수직계열화 가능  
- 결론: 1) Sectio n232의 핵심은 태양광 모듈 산업 보호를 넘어 폴리실리콘-잉곳-웨이퍼-셀로 이어지는 Upstream 공급망 재건. 
특히, 폴리실리콘 생산 기반은 반도체용 Upstream(초고순도폴리실리콘/웨이퍼 등) 구축의 핵심이기 때문. 태양광 프로젝트 
수익성 악화 리스크에도 불구, AI/반도체 공급망 구축이 국가 안보 상 더 중요하다고 판단한 셈 2) 이에 따라, Upsteam에 더 
높은 제조 인센티브와 수익성 보장. 특히, 웨이퍼는 MIP 적용 시 3~4배 가격 상승효과 발생 3) 즉, 이번 정책의 최대 수혜는 
셀/모듈업체보다는 폴리실리콘/웨이퍼 업체 4) 폴리실리콘/웨이퍼 등 Upstream을 중심으로 반도체 확장을 꾀하는 OCI홀딩스가 
최대 수혜. 실제, MIP 적용 시 이익 레버리지 효과 극대화 예상. 특히, 웨이퍼 사업은 이번 정책을 계기로 가치 재평가 요인이 될 
가능성 높으며 미국 내 추가 증설 가능성도 배제할 수 없어 5) 한화솔루션은 미국 내 수직계열화 완성으로 외부 원료 조달하는 
경쟁사 대비 압도적 경쟁력 보유할 것 5) Top Picks는 OCI홀딩스, 한화솔루션

도표 45. 폴리실리콘 및 파생제품 관련 Section 232 결과 정리

자료: Sunsirs, 하나증권

Compliance Notice  투자등급 관련사항 및 투자의견 비율공시

 당사는 2026년 10월 12일 현재 해당회사의 지분을 1%이상 보유 하고 있지 않습
니다  
 본 자료를 작성한 애널리스트(윤재성)는 자료의 작성과 관련하여 외부의 압력이나 
부당한 간섭을 받지 않았으며, 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하여 신의성실 하게 
작성하였습니다. 
 본 자료는 기관투자가 등 제 3자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다.  
 본 조사분석자료의 작성과 관련하여 당사의 금융투자분석사 윤재성은 SK이노베이
션의 비용으로 호주 가스전 투어 행사에 참석한 사실이 있음을 고지합니다.  
 본 자료를 작성한 애널리스트(윤재성)는 2026년 10월 12일 현재 해당회사의 유가
증권을 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
 LG화학 은/는 IB업무관련 비밀유지약정 체결법인으로 해당 정보가 미공개 된 경우
임

 투자의견의 유효기간은 추천일 이후 12개월을 기준으로 적용 
 기업의 분류 
BUY(매수)_목표주가가 현주가 대비 15% 이상 상승 여력 
Neutral(중립)_목표주가가 현주가 대비 -15%~15% 등락 
Reduce(비중축소)_목표주가가 현주가 대비 -15% 이상 하락 가능 
 산업의 분류 
Overweight(비중확대)_업종지수가 현재지수 대비 15% 이상 상승 여력 
Neutral(중립)_업종지수가 현재지수 대비 -15%~15% 등락 
Underweight(비중축소)_업종지수가 현재지수 대비 -15% 이상 하락 가능 
투자등급 BUY(매수) Neutral(중립) Reduce(매도) 합계 
금융투자상품의 비율 98.18% 1.82% 0.00% 100% 
* 기준일: 2026년 10월 09일

본 조사자료는 고객의 투자에 정보를 제공할 목적으로 작성되었으며, 어떠한 경우에
도 무단 복제 및 배포 될 수 없습니다. 또한 본 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사가 신뢰
할 만한 자료 및 정보로 얻어진 것이나, 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없으므
로 투자자 자신의 판단과 책임하에 최종결정을 하시기 바랍니다. 따라서 어떠한 경
우에도 본 자료는 고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재의 증빙자료로 사
용될 수 없습니다.

### Page 1

9월 외국인 카지노 3사 실적 
9월 국내 외국인 카지노 3사 합산 드롭액 12,049억원(+2.4% YoY  이하 YoY), 순매출 
1,531억원(+1.5%), 홀드율 12.7%(-0.1%p) 기록. 동기간 마카오의 카지노 매출액(GGR)
은 181억파타카(-1.2% YoY) 기록하며 월드컵 영향이 있던 6~7월 이후에도 9월까지 전년 
대비 감소세가 이어짐. 이는 7월 24일 시행된 해외 신탁 과세 및 신고 강화 등 중국 당국의 
자본 규제 강화가 중국인 VIP의 베팅 심리를 일부 위축시킨 영향으로 판단. 10월 중국 국경
절 연휴 효과로 인해 9월 대비 GGR 반등이 예상되나, 중국 VIP 및 Premium Mass 고객
의 센티먼트가 단기간에 회복되기는 제한적일 것으로 전망 
롯데관광개발: 9월 드롭액 2,761억원(-5.8% YoY) , 순매출 291억원(-45.1%), 홀드율 
10.5%(-7.5%p), 방문객수는 66,393명(+12.4%) 기록. 제주드림타워 카지노 방문객이 사
상 최대치(월별 기준) 기록했음에도 불구하고 인당 드롭액과 홀드율이 하락한 영향으로 순매
출은 역성장. 9월 홀드율 하락은 일부 VIP의 고액 승리에 따른 일시적 변동성 확대로 향후 
베팅 모수가 누적되면서 대수의 법칙에 따라 홀드율은 정상 수준으로 회귀할 것으로 판단. 
따라서 단기적인 홀드율 변동보다는 드림타워 카지노 방문객 수의 견조한 성장과 4분기 제
주-중국 신규 노선 취항에 주목할 필요 
파라다이스: 9월 드롭액 6,051억원(+6.6%YoY), 순매출 867억원(+37.5%), 홀드율 
14.3%(+3.2%p), VIP방문일수 14,380(+5.2%) 기록. P-City의 9월 VIP방문일수가 
+11.2% YoY 증가하면서 26년들어 가장 높은 성장세를 시현 것이 고무적. 하얏트 리젠시 
가동률 상승에 따른 콤프 확대를 기반으로 VIP 모객력이 본격적으로 강화될 것으로 기대 
GKL: 9월 드롭액 3,237억원(+2.4% YoY), 순매출 373억원(+7.2%), 홀드율 11.5% 
(+0.5%p), VIP방문객 수는 14,131 (-4.7%) 기록. GKL이 강남점을 VIP 중심 플래그십으로 
유지하고 용산점을 매스 중심 점포로 재편하는 전략을 취함에 따라 강남점 VIP방문객 수는 
YoY +16% 성장한 반면 용산점은 -14% 감소. 다만 매스 중심 전략에도 불구 용산점 전체 
방문객 수 감소가 지속되고 있어 향후 용산점 방문객 수 반등 여부가 관건 
8월 외국인 관광객 수 성장세 견조 
8월 전체 외국인 관광객 수는 중화권 입국자 수 증가에 힘입어 224만명 기록(+23.3% 
YoY). 1~8월 누적 외국인 관광객 수는 1,505만명(+21.6%) 기록하며 현재 성장률 추세 감
안 시 연말까지 정부 목표치인 2,300만명(+21.5%) 달성할 것으로 전망. 주요 국적별로는 
중국 83만명(+36.9%), 일본 42만명(+10.5%), 대만 25만명(+28.0%), 기타 75만명
(+16.8%) 입국. 중국 국경절 연휴(10/1~7)와 일본 실버위크(9/19~23) 기간 주요 OTA 해
외여행 예약 순위에서 한국이 각각 1위를 기록한 만큼 핵심 인바운드 국가인 중국과 일본의 
수요 확대를 바탕으로 9~10월 외국인 관광객 수 역시 견조한 증가세를 이어갈 것으로 전망

종목 투자의견 목표주가 (원)

Appendix

Document 587: [產業] 정유화학 Weekly - 미국, 디젤 가격 잡기 - 유진투자증권

Source PDF: all report pdf/[產業] 정유화학 Weekly - 미국, 디젤 가격 잡기 - 유진투자증권.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 9
Status: ok

### Page 1

Weekly | 에너지 | 2026.10.07.

주간 주요 제품 가격변화 
◼ 정유: WTI(-2.8%), 휘발유(+3.6%), 등유(+2.1%), 경유(-2.0%), 고유황중 유(+2.3%) 
◼ 화학: 프로판(+4.7%), 납사(-3.2%), LDPE(+2.2%), 벤젠(+2.2%), 톨루엔(+2.1%) 
◼ 태양광: 알루미늄(-5.3%), 메탈실리콘(+0.7%), 이 외 변화 없음

단기 세금 인하보다 디젤 수출 제한 가능성 여전히 남아 있어 
10/5, 트럼프 미국 대통령은 착색 경유(red-dyed diesel)의 일반 도로 사용을 연말
까지 한시적으로 허용하는 행정명령에 서명했다. 중동·러시아발 공급 차질과 글로
벌 정제설비 부족으로 미국 디젤 가격이 6 달러/갤런을 넘어선 데 따른 대응이다. 
착색 경유는 일반 경유와 성분은 같지만 농기계·건설장비 등 비도로용으로 판매돼 
세금이 면제되는 제품이다. 미국 연방 디젤 소비세는 24.4센트/갤런, 10.2달러/배
럴이며, 주정부 세금과 부담금까지 포함하면 평균 세금 부담은 약 60 센트/갤런, 
약 25 달러/배럴에 달한다. 이번 조치는 착색 경유의 도로 사용에 따른 세금 납부
를 연말까지 유예하고 벌금을 부과하지 않는 방식이다. 
문제는 세금보다 공급이다. 착색 경유와 도로용 경유는 동일한 제품이어서 사용 
제한을 풀어도 미국 전체 디젤 공급량은 늘어나지 않는다. 소비자의 체감 가격을 
낮추는 효과는 있지만 낮은 재고와 정제설비 부족을 해결할 수는 없다. 이에 미국
은 앞서 유럽에 비축유 방출을 요구하며 디젤 수출 제한 가능성까지 거론했다. 
G7이 향후 4개월간 1억배럴의 원유·디젤 등 비축유를 방출하기로 합의하면서 수
출금지는 일단 보류됐지만, 실제 방출이 지연되거나 가격 안정 효과가 제한적일 
경우 관련 논의가 재개될 가능성은 남아 있다. 미국이 디젤 수출을 제한하면 걸프 
연안 정유사의 디젤 재고가 빠르게 쌓일 수 있다. 정유사는 휘발유·디젤·항공유를 
동시에 생산하기 때문에 디젤만 감산하기 어렵다. 결국 저장능력이 한계에 도달하
면 전체 가동률을 낮춰야 하고, 이는 휘발유와 항공유 공급까지 감소시키는 결과
로 이어질 수 있다. 디젤 가격을 낮추기 위한 정책이 오히려 휘발유 마진을 밀어 
올리는 역설적인 상황이다.  
현재 착색 경유 허용은 정제마진을 훼손하는 공급 확대책보다 소비자 가격을 낮
추기 위한 세제 조치에 가깝다. G7 비축유 방출로 디젤 수급이 안정되지 못할 경
우 미국의 수출 제한 재개→정유사 가동률 하락→휘발유 공급 축소→휘발유 마진 
상승으로 이어질 가능성도 주목할 필요가 있다. 
 주간 주요 뉴스 
G7, 비축 유 1 억배럴 방출 합의 
IEA 공조 방식으로 경유 및 원유 비 축 유 1 억
배럴을 4 개월 간 방출하고 회원국 간 에너지
제품 수출제한을 자제하기로 합의

사우디, 11월 아시아 OSP 인하 
아람코는 아 시 아 에 공급하는 Arab Light OSP
를 오만 및 두바이 평균 대비 배 럴 당 5달러 할
인, 운임 급등에 따른 구 매 자 부담 보전 차원

OPEC+, 11월 원유 생산 목표 유지 합의 
이란 전쟁으로 OPEC+ 일부 회원국의 원유 생
산이 상 당 한 규모로 중 단 된 상황에서 추가 쿼
터 변경 대신 기존 생 산 계 획 을 그대로 유지

중국 정유기업, 10월 석유제 품 수출 중단 
페 트 로 차 이 나 는 10 월 예정된 석유제품 선적 
일부 취소, Zhejiang P etrochemical 또한 국경절 
연휴 기간동안 수출 일정 미편성

이란, 호르무즈 재개방 불가 입장 재 확 인 
협상에서 제시한 미국의 해상봉쇄 해제, 동결
자금 반환, 이란산 원유 수출 제재 면제 등의 7
가지 조건들이 받 아 들 여 지 기 전 까 지 호르무즈 
해협을 다시 개 방 하 지 않겠다고 주장

Compliance Notice 
당사는 자료 작성일 기준으로 지난 3 개월 간 해당종목에 대해서 유가증권 발행에 참여한 적이 없습니다 
당사는 본 자료 발간일을 기준으로 해당종목의 주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다  
당사는 동 자료를 기관투자가 또는 제 3 자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다 
조사분석담당자는 자료작성일 현재 동 종목과 관련하여 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 조사분석담당자 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성되었음을 확인합니다 
동 자료는 당사의 제작물로서 모든 저작권은 당사에게 있습니다 
동 자료는 당사의 동의 없이 어떠한 경우에도 어떠한 형태로든 복제, 배포, 전송, 변형, 대여할 수 없습니다 
동 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료 및 정보로부터 얻어진 것이나, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 어떠
한 경우에 동 자료는 고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙자료로 사용될 수 없습니다

투자기간 및 투자등급/투자의견 비율 
종목추천 및 업종추천 투자기간: 12개월  (추천기준일 종가대비 추천종목의 예상 목표수익률을 의미함) 당사 투자의견 비율(%) 
ㆍSTRONG BUY(매수) 추천기준일 종가대비 +50%이상 3% 
ㆍBUY(매수) 추천기준일 종가대비 +15%이상 ~ +50%미만 94% 
ㆍHOLD(중립) 추천기준일 종가대비 -10%이상 ∼ +15%미만 3% 
ㆍREDUCE(매도) 추천기준일 종가대비 -10%미만 0%

### Page 1

9월 국내 일평균거래대금 40.1조원, MoM -19.0% 
9월 일평균거래대금은 40.1조원으로 전월 대비 19.0% 감소 (코스피 -24.5%, 코스닥 
+8.7%) 하였는데 여전히 개인투자자 회전율이 하락하고 있어 거래대금 역시 부진한 수준을 
보이고 있다고 판단된다. 다만 코스닥 회전율은 전월 대비 개선되었으며 현재 회전율 수준
이 감안 시 향후 투자자들의 투자심리가 개선됨에 따라 회전율 상승이 점진적으로 기대되는 
상황이다.  
해외 결제금액은 전월 대비 16.0% 감소하였으며 해외 반도체 기업 및 글로벌우주 기업의 
주가가 부진하며 거래가 감소한 것에 기인한다.  
신용공여 잔고는 전월 대비 0.4% 증가한 59.0조원을 기록했는데 이는 예탁증권담보융자의 
증가에 기인하며 신용융자 잔고는 전월 대비 1.0% 감소하였다. 다만 감소 추세가 완만해지
고 있으며 점진적인 위험자산 선호심리 개선이 예상된다. 
고객예탁금은 108조원으로 전월 대비 8.0% 증가하였는데 증시 대기자금인 만큼향후 증시
로 유입될 자금이 늘어나고 있다는 점은 주식시장에 긍정적인 영향으로 생각된다.  
9월 일평균거래대금은 전월대비 감소했지만 코스닥 시장의 회전율이 반등한 점과 신용융자 
및 고객예탁금의 증가를 감안 시 투자자들의 위험선로 심리는 점진적으로 회복하고 있다고 
판단되며 현재 코스피 및 코스닥 시장 회전율은 추세적 저점 수준으로 추가적인 하락은 제
한적으로 생각되며 일평균거래대금은 9월을 저점으로 4분기 중으로 점진적 회복이 예상된
다.

투자전략 
증권업종에 대해 Overweight를 유지한다. Overweight를 유지하는 이유는 1) 증시가 점진
적으로회복하고 있어 거래대금 증가에 긍정적 요인으로 작용이 예상되며, 2) 개인투자자 매
매비중이 50% 초반에서 횡보하고 있다는 점에서 개인투자자 회전율 상승이 기대되기 때문
이다. 반도체 쏠림 현상에 대한 우려는 있으나 연말로 갈수록 증권사들의 배당매력 부각 및 
반도체 쏠림 강도 축소로 증권업종 주가 상승은 가능할 것으로 판단된다.

Top pick: 키움증권 
증권업종 Top pick으로는 코스닥 시장 민감도가 높은 키움증권을 제시한다. 배당수익률은 
7.4% 수준이다.

### Page 1

사라진 금구은십(金九銀十), 중국 소비의 잃어버린 가을 
부동산 부진에 막힌 소비 회복 
중국 AI·로봇 산업은 빠른 산업 고도화와 정책 지원에 힘입어 새로운 성장동력으로 부상하고 있는 반면, 소비 관
련주는 정부의 잇따른 부양책에도 소비심리 회복이 지연되며 부진한 흐름이 이어지고 있다. 소비 부문의 부진은 
기업 실적에서도 확인된다. 2분기 기준 MSCI China의 업종별 실적 서프라이즈는 전체 +4.8%, IT +9.0%를 기록
한 반면 필수소비재는 -10.4%로 시장 기대를 크게 하회했다. 중국의 전통 소비재를 대표하는 귀주모태의 상반
기 순이익은 445억원으로 전년동기대비 1.95% 감소하며  2014년 이후 10년 만에 역성장을 기록했다. 
그림 1. MSCI 중국 경기·필수소비재 및 정보기술 지수 추이  그림 2. MSCI China 업종별 2분기 실적 서프라이즈

자료: Bloomberg, IBK투자증권

자료: I/B/E/S, MSCI, IBK투자증권

중국 소비를 제약하는 근본적인 요인은 부동산 경기 부진이다. 중국 가계자산에서 부동산이 차지하는 비중은  
50% 이상으로 다른 주요국보다 높은 수준이다. 이에 따라 주택가격 하락은 가계의 순자산을 감소시키는 동시에 
향후 자산가격에 대한 불확실성을 높여 소비심리를 위축시키는 요인으로 작용한다. 특히 부동산 가격의 하락세
가 장기화되면서 가계는 지출보다 저축을 선호하고 있으며, 주택 거래 감소는 가구·가전 등 연관 소비까지 위축
시키고 있다. 
문제는 부동산 경기 부진에 따른 소비 위축을 정책 지원만으로 상쇄하기도 쉽지 않다는 점이다. 중국 정부는 소
비 회복을 위해 이구환신 정책을 이어가고 있지만 올해 정책 여력은 지난해보다 오히려 축소됐다. 2025년 3,000
억 위안이었던 지원 규모는 올해 2,500억 위안으로 감소했다. 스마트폰과 태블릿 등 디지털·스마트 제품으로 지
원 범위가 넓어졌지만 전체 재원이 감소하면서 품목 확대에 따른 추가적인 소비 진작 효과는 제한될 가능성이 있
다. 특히 지난해 이구환신 정책이 자동차와 가전 등 내구재 소비를 상당 부분 앞당겼다는 점도 올해 정책 효과를 
제약하는 요인이다. 이미 보조금을 활용해 제품을 교체한 가계의 재구매 수요가 크지 않은 만큼 동일한 방식의 
정책을 반복하더라도 지난해와 같은 소비 증가 효과를 기대하기 어렵다.

그림 3. 중국 월별 소매판매 증가율 추이  그림 4. 중국 소비자심리지수 및 부동산 경기지수

자료: 중국 국가통계국, Bloomberg, IBK투자증권  자료: Bloomberg, IBK투자증권

금구은십(金九銀十)은 이제 옛말 
금구은십은 ‘금 같은 9월, 은 같은 10월’이라는 뜻으로, 중추절과 국경절 등이 위치한 9~10월의 전통적인 소비 
성수기를 의미한다. 따라서 연휴를 맞아 중국 소비의 회복을 기대하는 시선도 있으나 최근에는 이러한 계절적 소
비 특수도 약화되는 모습이다. 국경절 연휴 기간 관광객 수가 코로나19 이전 수준을 넘어서는 등 여행 수요는 빠
르게 회복됐지만, 2025년까지 소비 지출의 회복은 상대적으로 더뎠다. 2025년 1인당 관광 지출은 911위안에 그
쳤으며, 연휴 일수를 감안한 일평균 1인당 지출도 114위안으로 코로나 이전인 2019년의 119위안을 하회했다. 국
경절 소매·외식기업 매출 증가율 역시 둔화되면서 이동량 증가가 소비 지출 확대로 온전히 이어지지 못했다. 
서비스 소비에 대한 지원 강도 역시 약해졌다. 국경절을 맞아 각 지방정부가 소비쿠폰 및 보조금을 지급하며 문
화·관광·외식 등 서비스 소비 부양에 나서고 있으나, 지원 규모는 지난해 4.8억 위안 이상에서 올해 3.1억 위안 
이상으로 축소됐다. 소비쿠폰은 특정 기간의 지출을 유도하는 데에는 효과적이지만 가계소득이나 순자산을 직접 
개선하는 정책은 아니라는 점에서 효과의 지속성에도 한계가 있다. 결국 올해 소비 부양책은 정책 규모 축소와 
지난해 소비의 선반영 효과, 부동산발 자산효과 약화가 맞물리면서 중국 소비의 추세적인 회복을 이끌기에는 부
족할 것으로 판단한다. 
그림 5. 국경절 연휴 기간 일평균 지출액 및 이동 인구  그림 6. 국경절 연휴 기간 소매·외식기업 매출 증가율

자료: 중국 상무부, 중국 교통운수부, IBK투자증권  자료: 중국 상무부, IBK투자증권

Top 5 ngành tăng / giảm  VNINDEX tăng điểm trong tuần qua, +68.24 điểm tương ứng +4.00%, với thanh khoản cải 
thiện. Thiết bị điện, Khách sạn - Nhà hàng và Thiết bị dịch vụ y tế là các nhóm ngành có 
mức tăng rõ rệt nhất trong tuần. Khối ngoại duy trì bán ròng so với xu hướng của tuần 
trước với giá trị -966 tỷ đồng, tập trung ở các mã FPT, ACB và VCB; mặt khác, tự doanh duy 
trì mua ròng ở mức 137 tỷ đồng.

Ở một khía cạnh khác, khối ngoại đảo chiều mua ròng 1,335 HĐTL VN30F1M trong tuần 
qua nhưng đang duy trì vị thế bán lũy kế -3,049 hợp đồng. Tự doanh đảo chiều mua ròng 
1,815 hợp đồng nhưng đang duy trì trạng thái lũy kế bán ròng -6,000 hợp đồng.

TTCK Việt Nam hồi phục trở lại sau bốn tuần giảm liên tiếp, khi VN-Index bật tăng mạnh 
từ vùng hỗ trợ quanh 1.700 điểm với thanh khoản cải thiện. Sau nhịp điều chỉnh kéo dài 
khiến mặt bằng định giá về vùng hấp dẫn, lực cầu bắt đáy đã nhập cuộc quyết liệt hơn, 
với phiên 21/10 tăng gần 47 điểm — một trong những phiên tăng mạnh nhất nhiều tháng 
gần đây. Điểm tích cực và đáng chú ý nhất là khối ngoại đã xuất hiện hai phiên đảo chiều 
mua ròng trở lại sau khoảng 20 phiên bán ròng liên tiếp, tập trung vào nhóm Bluechip 
như VIC, TCB, VHM, MSN — tín hiệu đầu tiên cho thấy áp lực rút vốn ngoại có thể đang dần 
hạ nhiệt. Thông tin tích cực tác động lên thị trường đến từ các chỉ đạo của NHNN và Chính 
phủ về việc hạ mặt bằng lãi suất để hỗ trợ tăng trưởng kinh tế. Bên cạnh đó, mùa báo cáo 
KQKD quý 3 khả quan đang diễn ra cũng là yếu tố hỗ trợ xu hướng thị trường chung.

Nguồn: V_KBSource

Triển vọng kinh tế 4Q2026 
Giữ nhịp tăng trưởng, vững vàng về itch

Một số dự báo chính của KBSV về kinh tế Việt Nam trong năm 2026:

1) Tăng trưởng GDP năm 2026 được dự báo sẽ đạt 9.0%, với động lực chính 
đến từ định hướng đẩy mạnh giải ngân đầu tư công của Chính phủ, dòng 
vốn FDI duy trì tích cực và hoạt động sản xuất công nghiệp phục hồi theo 
xuất khẩu. Về rủi ro, chúng tôi cho rằng Việt Nam sẽ tiếp tục đối mặt với 
một số yếu tố bất định từ bên ngoài, bao gồm: rủi ro xung đột Mỹ – Iran 
kéo dài hơn dự kiến, xu hướng tăng của mặt bằng lãi suất toàn cầu gây gia 
tăng áp lực tài chính và kéo giảm tổng cầu, cùng nguy cơ vỡ bong bóng AI.

2) Chúng tôi nâng dự báo lạm phát bình quân năm 2026 của Việt Nam lên 
mức 4.7% YoY, cao hơn mục tiêu kiểm soát lạm phát của Chính phủ, dựa 
trên kịch bản dự báo thận trọng hơn về diễn biến xung đột địa chính trị và 
giá năng lượng thế giới trong ngắn hạn.

3) Mặt bằng lãi suất trong quý IV được dự báo sẽ ổn định quanh vùng hiện 
tại và không xuất hiện thêm nhịp tăng đột biến, nhờ dòng tiền cải thiện 
từ giải ngân đầu tư công và kỳ vọng có thêm các biện pháp hỗ trợ thanh 
khoản trung dài hạn từ phía Chính phủ và NHNN.

4) Tỷ giá USD/VND trong quý cuối năm có thể chịu một số sức ép do Fed dự 
báo còn thêm một lần tăng lãi suất nữa trong năm. Dù vậy, chúng tôi cho 
rằng tỷ giá USD/VND khó tăng mạnh mà chỉ quay lại mức đầu năm, dựa 
trên kỳ vọng chỉ số DXY không biến động mạnh, nguồn cung ngoại tệ cải 
thiện (FDI giải ngân, kiều hối, nâng hạng TTCK) và chênh lệch lãi suất 
VND–USD duy trì trạng thái dương.

Tham khảo nội dung chi tiết trong báo cáo tại đây.

21/10/2026

CTCP VÀNG BẠC ĐÁ QUÝ PHÚ NHUẬN (PNJ)  
Cập nhật ĐHĐCĐ bất thường 21/10/2026

Tái cơ cấu để xử lý áp lực thanh khoản  
— ĐHĐCĐ bất thường PNJ ngày 21/10/2026 đã thông qua điều chỉnh kế hoạch kinh 
doanh và phương án phát hành riêng lẻ để bổ sung vốn tái cấu trúc. Ban lãnh đạo đặt 
ra các vấn đề trọng yếu là đáp ứng nhu cầu thanh khoản và chuẩn bị nguồn lực để thực 
hiện các cam kết thu đổi đã tích lũy trong quá khứ. Bên cạnh đó, các cổ đông tham dự 
cũng đặt các câu hỏi liên quan đến tình hình hoạt động qua các điểm chính (1) dòng 
tiền hoạt động kinh doanh; (2) diễn biến thị trường sau khi PNJ áp dụng chính sách thu 
đổi mới; (3) hiệu quả hoạt động sau tái cấu trúc.

Nội dung chính tại đại hội 
— Theo Ban lãnh đạo, thông tin liên quan đến P-Lab từ đầu tháng 7 khiến khách hàng gia 
tăng bán lại sản phẩm. Tổng giá trị thu đổi và mua lại trong tháng đạt gần 6,000 tỷ VND, 
so với khoảng 7,000 tỷ VND trong cả năm 2025. Theo BCTC riêng, nguồn tiền khả dụng 
giảm từ khoảng 3,700 tỷ VND cuối Q2/2026 xuống 770 tỷ VND cuối Q3/2026, đã bao 
gồm khoản vay 700 tỷ VND từ Chủ tịch HĐQT. Tỷ trọng kim cương và trang sức kim 
cương trong tổng tồn kho tăng từ 41% lên 52%. 
— Q3/2026 ghi nhận lỗ sau thuế 258 tỷ VND trước phần tăng trích lập dự phòng. Để giảm 
áp lực thanh toán, PNJ kéo dài thời gian chi trả đối với kim cương lên 120 ngày từ tháng 
07/2026. Thay đổi chính sách cũng ảnh hưởng đến niềm tin khách hàng và sức mua, cụ 
thể doanh thu và lợi nhuận gộp Q3/2026 đạt lần lượt 6,995/609 tỷ VND, giảm 14%/62% 
YoY. Chi phí bán hàng và quản lý giảm 13% YoY chưa đủ bù đắp mức giảm lợi nhuận 
gộp, khiến PNJ lỗ sau thuế 258 tỷ VND, so với lãi 496 tỷ VND trong Q3/2025. 
— PNJ nâng giả định tỷ lệ thu đổi trên giá trị thu đổi còn lại lên 80%, so với 28.8% đối với 
nhóm sản phẩm giai đoạn 2020–2026 trong ước tính tại BCTC bán niên 2026. Tổng dự 
phòng tổn thất dự kiến tăng từ 2,267 tỷ VND lên 7,071 tỷ VND. Kế hoạch điều chỉnh ghi 
nhận doanh thu 39,057 tỷ VND, lỗ trước thuế 6,059 tỷ VND và lỗ sau thuế 6,271 tỷ VND. 
Theo Ban lãnh đạo, LNST loại trừ dự phòng ước khoảng 800 tỷ VND, chủ yếu nhờ kết 
quả Q1/2026. Khoản dự phòng phản ánh tổn thất ước tính cho toàn bộ kịch bản, không 
đồng nghĩa toàn bộ giá trị sẽ được chi trả ngay trong kỳ. 
— Giá trị cam kết thu đổi tích lũy đối với kim cương và trang sức kim cương tại cuối 
Q3/2026 được PNJ ước tính khoảng 21,000 tỷ VND. Với hàng đã bán, doanh nghiệp dự 
kiến chuyển đổi quyền lợi khách hàng sang sản phẩm và các chương trình ưu đãi. Với 
sản phẩm bán mới, chính sách thu đổi định hướng theo giá thị trường tại thời điểm 
giao dịch, thay cho tỷ lệ cố định trên giá trị hóa đơn, nhằm hạn chế tiếp tục tích lũy 
nghĩa vụ tiềm tàng trong tương lai theo cơ chế cũ. 
Tham khảo nội dung chi tiết trong báo cáo tại đây.

Ngưỡng kỹ thuật

VNINDEX hình thành cây nến xanh thân dài trên khung tuần và đáng chú ý là cho phản ứng 
bật tăng ngay tại cạnh dưới của mẫu hình tam giác đi ngang trung hạn, cho thấy tín hiệu 
tích cực đã quay trở lại với xu hướng ngắn hạn. Trên khung ngày, sau 2 phiên điều chỉnh, 
rung lắc vào đầu tuần, chỉ số cũng đảo chiều tăng điểm khá dốc, với 3 cây nến xanh liên 
tiếp với thanh khoản được tăng cường, củng cố thêm tín hiệu xác nhận cho đà hồi phục. 
Sau nhịp bật tăng mạnh từ đáy, VNINDEX được dự báo sẽ sớm gặp phải áp lực điều chỉnh 
khi quay lên điểm giữa của dải đi ngang theo mẫu hình tam giác, tương ứng vùng kháng cự 
kế tiếp tại quanh 1810. Tuy nhiên, xu hướng ngắn hạn vẫn có phần nghiêng về chiều tăng, 
với tín hiệu xác nhận đáy của một số mã cổ phiếu sau quá trình sụt giảm kéo dài.

(Ghi chú: NĐT có thể dựa theo nhận định xu hướng thị trường để tham khảo khuyến nghị 
hành động tương ứng trong bảng phía trên)

- Danh mục tập hợp các cổ phiếu tiềm năng nhất được KBSV 
lựa chọn và tái cơ cấu theo tháng.

- Danh mục giả định luôn duy trì tỷ trọng 100% là cổ phiếu với 
trọng số được phân bổ đều giữa các mã.

- NĐT được khuyến nghị mua tại mức giá bằng hoặc thấp hơn 
giá khuyến nghị mua. 
- Lợi nhuận kỳ vọng được tính theo giá khuyến nghị mua.

Document 718: Fed holds rates steady despite inflation being too high

Source PDF: all report pdf/Fed holds rates steady despite inflation being too high.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-28
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

Fed Chair Kevin Warsh acknowledged that progress is being
made, but inﬂation remains too high and a December FOMC
rate hike remains our call

James Knightley, Padhraic Garvey, CFA and Chris Turner
Federal Reserve Chair Warsh announced that interest rates remain unchanged
10-2 vote to keep policy unchanged
The Federal Reserve voted 10-2 in favour of leaving the Fed funds
target rate range at 3.75%-4%. Only 2bp of a potential 25bp hike
was priced ahead of the decision and only 6 of 97 organisations
polled by Bloomberg predicted a hike, so this was no surprise and
market reaction has been limited.
Senior Fed oﬃcials, including Vice Chairs Philip Jeﬀerson and
Michelle Bowman and Governor Chris Waller, suggested ahead of
the decision that they were prepared to move cautiously, seeing
little need for back-to-back rate hikes after September's policy
change. Nonetheless, Beth Hammack and Lisa Cook both felt that
the current situation warranted additional action. Cook’s vote for a

### Page 2

hike was a surprise given the general sense that she was a centrist,
and it will do nothing to improve her fraught relationship with
President Trump. Instead, we had suspected it would be one or both
of Neel Kashkari and Lorrie Logan (who both also dissented in July)
that would join Hammack in voting in favour of a further rate hike.
There aren’t many changes in the accompanying press release,
with the economy described as “expanding at a strong pace” and

“inﬂation remains elevated”. There was no pre-commitment to
further action, with it being the data that “will help the Committee
determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return
of inﬂation to the Committee's 2 percent goal."
At the press conference, Kevin Warsh oﬀered no real guidance other
than to say that inﬂation remains too high, but the overwhelming
consensus in the FOMC is that the “medicine” is starting to work.
Moreover, the Fed will deliver price stability, “no ifs, ands or buts." He

did state that from next September one FOMC meeting won’t have
a rate vote, leaving it to focus exclusively on monetary policy
strategy. From 2028, this will become two meetings, leaving only
six meetings at which interest rate decisions will be made.

December rate hike remains the most likely

### Page 3

There is the valid question that, given a majority of FOMC members
project a rate hike before the end of the year, why didn’t they take
that decision today? Kevin Warsh’s response was that he himself
didn’t make a prediction and, in any case, they removed some
policy accommodation in September. Inﬂation is “going the right
way” and he is encouraged by the progress made.
We will have two jobs reports and one inﬂation print ahead of the
December decision. The November CPI will be released the day after

the Fed meeting, and oﬃcials will almost certainly get to see it. As
such, they will have more information than the market does,
meaning a surprise can’t be completely ruled out. For now, a
December hike looks a solid bet, which would leave the Fed funds
target rate range at 4.00%-4.25%.
The sharp move higher in Treasury yields and the ﬁrmer dollar are
tightening ﬁnancial conditions to a degree, but our forecast path
for the Fed assumes that in the couple of months following the

midterm elections we see an agreement that improves the ﬂow of
energy from the Persian Gulf, but signiﬁcantly, allows for a sharp
reduction in shipping and insurance costs. It's the latter point that is
crucial to delivering lower pump prices at a time when reﬁneries are
working at capacity.
If correct, this should meaningfully ease inﬂation pressures, and
with US wage growth (the biggest cost input for corporate America)
looking very benign at just 3% and the largest component of the

inﬂation basket – housing at 35% by weight – set to experience
more disinﬂation, we believe overall US inﬂation can fall back to 2%
in the second half of 2027.
Hence, we subsequently anticipate a prolonged pause through to
December 2027, when we have that December 2026 hike being
reversed. This is in line with the current consensus forecast, while
the Fed’s own projections (its central forecast) see one hike this year
with rates left unchanged throughout 2027.

The lean hawkish is enough to validate the
break back above 5% for the 10yr

Hardly a word in the short statement that is not leaning hawkish.
But no hike. Prior to the FOMC meeting, it was remarkable how the
10yr yield had been clinging to the 4.99% level. It was like a
comfort level; technically below 5%, but not too far below in case it
needs to turn tail and shoot back up again. It seemed that the US
10yr was waiting to tick oﬀ this outcome before deviating too
much from 5%. Well, it’s now had the outcome. The initial reaction

was up. But the reaction to Chair Warsh’s words at the press
conference was to revert down to 5%, and brieﬂy below. This can be
rationalised by Warsh’s comforting words on inﬂation expectations,
which he deemed to have been tamed somewhat.
That all being said, our interpretation is that this Fed still has a job
to do if the type of price stability that Chair Warsh has described is
to be achieved. He has described price stability as strictly 2%, and

he seems to be serious about hitting that. This is good from a
protective sense for the back end. At the same time, it’s tough to
view the current set-up of still elevated printed inﬂation and a Fed
on hold as all that protective. Also, inﬂation expectations in fact
rose post the Fed decision and subsequent Warsh commentary. We
glean from this enough to validate the move back above 5% on the
10yr. We maintain our view that we’re on a journey back up in the

direction of 5.25%.
The implementation note was broadly unchanged in terms of
action. The various implementation rates are unchanged, as are the
implementation mechanisms. And the Fed will continue to reinvest
principal payments from its MBS securities into Treasury bills. We’re
likely still some way from the Fed spelling out a plan to shrink its
balance sheet, which we know remains a policy objective for Chair
Warsh. We expect recommendations from the various committees

to surface closer to the turn of the year. Meanwhile, liquidity
conditions are stable, with repo behaving far better than it did a
year ago.
Dollar softens a touch
Perhaps it was a belated acknowledgement that there would not be
forward guidance on a December rate hike, but the lack of clarity

Document 726: France_ double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in the rise in bankruptcies and stable non-performing loans ratio (2026-10-08)

Source PDF: all report pdf/France_ double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in the rise in bankruptcies and stable non-performing loans ratio (2026-10-08).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-08
Pages: 1
Status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 8 October 2026

EcoFlash

France: double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in the rise in bankruptcies and 
stable non-performing loans ratio

Business creations rose by 12% y/y over the first eight months of 2026 1. This is significantly higher than the 5.3% y/y 
growth recorded in 2025. This increase was driven by sectors linked to information and communication technologies.

Meanwhile, business bankruptcies rose by 5.3% y/y between January and July, according to data from the Banque de 
France (compared with the 3.6% y/y increase for the whole of 2025). The rise was more pronounced in the 1st quarter (+8% y/y) 
before slowing down. This slowdown could persist, as, according to Altares, payment delays have fallen to an average of 12.3 
days in the second quarter of 2026, compared with 14.1 days a year earlier (and 13.1 days for the European average).

Rising bankruptcies are more heavily affecting the services sector, which is also experiencing slightly stronger growth 
in business creations. This correlation is particularly noticeable in the retail sector, as business creations have risen by 13% 
since the start of 2026, while bankruptcies are up by 3%. In construction and manufacturing, business bankruptcies have been 
falling since the second quarter of 2026.

The average ratio of non -performing corporate loans has remained stable at a moderately high level for the past year  
(4.0% of total loans and advances in Q2 2026). In construction, where activity is more cyclical than in other sectors, the ra tio of 
non-performing loans has reached its highest level since 2019, standing at 9.5%.

The proportion of loans that have deteriorated but are not yet non-performing (“Stage 2” according to IFRS 9 terminology) 
is stable, standing at 15.4% q/q, and remains below its all -time high (16.1%). This indicator, which helps to anticipate trends in 
non-performing loans, is relatively encouraging.

Despite a significant increase since 2021, the economic weight of bankruptcies remains, on average, below its peak in 
Q1 2025 (0.53 compared with 0.61, respectively). This indicator generally allows bankruptcies to be weighted based on the size 
of the businesses. However, the economic weight of bankrupted small businesses is at a historically high level (2.11).

Take a look at our EcoCharts on trends in business creations and bankruptcies in France and the 
associated risks. It will be updated on a quarterly basis.

Economy 
Kicking and screaming: Child care costs persist 
20 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Child care remains a growing financial burden for many households, even as growth in the median payment has moderated, 
according to Bank of America payments data. More families are paying more than $2,000 per month for care, suggesting 
affordability pressures are becoming increasingly concentrated among households facing the highest expenses. 
• Child care cost pressures vary significantly across the country. Nashville and Jacksonville, FL posted the fastest year-over-year 
growth among major metro areas, while New York and Washington DC saw the biggest decrease in median payment growth, 
according to Bank of America payments data. 
• Limited supply may continue to constrain access to child care for some households. Child care employment fell 0.7% year-over-
year in September, suggesting provider capacity remains under pressure and could continue to affect household finances and 
labor force participation.

Child care costs outpace rent and medical services 
Child care remains a significant expense for many families. In September, the growth in child care and other school-related fees 
surpassed other major household expenses such as rent and medical services, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) 
(Exhibit 1). 
Yet, while child care prices continue to rise in official inflation data, median household payments have largely moderated since 
February, according to Bank of America data (Exhibit 2). More importantly, the growth in the number of households paying for 
child care has fallen since the start of 2025.

The decline in the number of households paying for child care may also reflect families seeking alternatives to formal care

More families face larger monthly bills 
Yet even as fewer households pay for child care, the share of those shelling out more than $2,000 per month has increased 
substantially (Exhibit 4). In fact, it has more than doubled since 2023, reaching 7.7% in 2026 year-to-date. Meanwhile, the share

Rising costs take a bigger bite out of households’ paychecks 
What makes child care “affordable”? According to the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, child care is considered 
affordable if it accounts for no more than 7% of family income. Yet, in Bank of America data, the average child care cost was 8% 
in September.  
While an 8% share may not appear dramatically above the federal affordability benchmark of 7%, it represents an average across 
households’ income levels. For families in high-cost metro areas or those paying more than $2,000 per month, the burden is 
likely considerably higher.

Where child care costs are climbing 
Child care pressures also differ substantially by location. In Bank of America payments data, Nashville posted the fastest YoY

Fewer providers, higher costs 
As of September 2026, employment in child care services was down 0.7% from a year earlier, suggesting the sector’s workforce 
recovery has stalled. Fewer workers can constrain the number of available places or the hours providers offer. That may leave 
families facing longer searches, fewer workable arrangements or continued price pressure. In turn, access to care can influence 
parents’ ability to enter, remain in or expand their participation in the workforce. 
Exhibit 7: Employment within child care services fell 0.7% YoY in September 
Employees by sector (monthly, seasonally adjusted, YoY%)

Source: Haver Analytics  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
What are the economic implications of rising child care costs? 
Child care costs may have important labor market implications for families with young children. Of those households paying for 
child care in 2026, more than half of them were Older Millennials (52%), followed by Younger Millennials (20%) and Gen X (16%), 
according to Bank of America payments data. This means child care costs weigh more heavily on Older Millennials. 
The share of Older Millennial households receiving more than one paycheck has continued to grow from a year earlier, but the 
pace of that improvement has moderated over the past three months (Exhibit 8). Given that this group accounts for the majority 
of households paying for child care in Bank of America payments data, they may offer early signs of how affordability affects 
their employment decisions. 
Exhibit 8: The share of Older Millennial households paying for child care that have more than one person with a paycheck has declined since 2021  
Share of households with a child care payment with more than one person with a paycheck (%, monthly, three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Child care challenges can weigh on women’s employment 
According to data from the American Time Use Survey, women continue to perform significantly more child care and household 
work than men, even as their participation in paid work has increased. This suggests that persistent gaps in access and 
affordability may have disproportionate consequences for women’s employment. Indeed, the percentage of women who cited 
child care as a reason for temporary unemployment was nearly three times higher than men’s in 2026, though the average fell 
from 2025, according to data from the BLS. 
Yet labor force participation data indicates a more nuanced picture. Despite persistent child care pressures, mothers are 
participating in the workforce at rates that remain above their 2019 averages.

Methodology 
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Bank of America credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card holders 
making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards is excluded. Data regarding 
merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial services 
companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System (NAICS), 
which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
If applicable, the consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level.  
If applicable, any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a 
deposit account or credit card. Any reference to aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-
peer, cash and checks.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

Document 769: Preview of 2026 New Zealand General Election (2026-10-30)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Preview of 2026 New Zealand General Election (2026-10-30).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-30
Pages: 13
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• Polling points to a very close and unusually
fragmented election, with the outcome
likely to depend on the performance of the
Opportunity Party and whether any party
achieves “overhang” seats.
• While either Labour or National will lead the
next government, minor parties are likely to
have unprecedented representation in the next
government. This makes it harder to assess
the mix of policies that will emerge from

coalition negotiations.
• Compared to the status quo, a returned
National-led government with stronger
ACT and NZ First representation may be
more aggressive on some combination
of expenditure restraint, welfare and
immigration, resource development
and deregulation.
• A Labour-Green-Opportunity administration
would probably deliver more public-service
and social investment, a broader tax base,
stronger climate and labour regulation and
likely greater borrowing for infrastructure.

A centre-left government may find it hard to
resist pressure to lift public service salaries
and the minimum wage in response to fuel-
driven inflation.
• The fiscal starting point acts as a constraint:
the PREFU projects that OBEGALx will remain
in deficit until 2028/29 and achieving that
outcome will require exceptional spending
control in the current environment.
• Whichever government is elected will need
to make hard trade-offs, or raise additional

revenue or debt. The latter will be costly with
global investors requiring higher interest rates
to fund government (and other) debt.
• A key non-fiscal outcome of a change
in government would likely be the
implementation of the Labour Party’s
proposal to restore the RBNZ’s dual inflation-
employment mandate.
The election appears close, but the bigger
story is fragmentation.
New Zealand goes to the ballot box on 7 November
(although advance voting started on 26 October). The

campaign is taking place against a mixed economic
backdrop: activity has recovered somewhat, but
households remain sensitive to high living costs and a
weak housing market; the fiscal accounts have improved
relative to the Budget but still show deficits until 2028/29.
That combination gives the various parties room to
argue about priorities, but little room to avoid difficult
trade-offs. Additional spending – which may be difficult
to avoid in the current environment – will require either

additional revenue or a willingness to accept additional
debt issuance at a time when international investors are
seeking greater compensation to fund government debt
(and other).
The striking feature of this campaign is the reduced
dominance of the two major parties. Across the major
polls, neither traditional centre-left or centre-right
blocs can be confident of governing alone, and the
composition of the minor-party vote will determine both
who governs and how. Because the Opportunity Party

sits near the threshold, a movement of only a few tenths
of a percentage point could alter the allocation of several
seats. In addition, any “overhang” created by Te Pāti Māori
or the National Party winning more electorates than their
party vote entitlement could also move the majority line
above the usual 61 seats.
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
Preview of 2026 General Election.
27 Oct 2026 |  Westpac Economics Team | westpac.co.nz/economics | economics@westpac.co.nz

Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
Together with uncertainty about voter turnout –
especially considering changes in voter registration rules
– the outcome of this General Election is difficult to call.
With major-party support unusually low and the minor
parties offering distinct, sometimes internally conflicting
programmes, the shape of the next government will be
determined not only at the ballot box on 7 November,
but at the negotiating table afterwards. Moreover, if the

results are especially tight, the outcome may not be clear
until sometime after the official results are declared on
27 November. And a hung parliament – where neither the
centre-left nor centre-right bloc can command a majority
– cannot be entirely ruled out either.
How New Zealand’s electoral system works.
New Zealand’s electoral system uses Mixed Member
Proportional representation (or MMP). Voters receive
two votes: an electorate vote for a local candidate and a

party vote for a political party. The electorate candidate
with the most votes wins the local seat. The party vote is
the main determinant of each qualifying party’s overall
representation in Parliament.
Parliament normally has 120 seats. For the 2026
election there are 71 electorates, comprising 64 general
electorates and seven Māori electorates, with the
remaining seats ordinarily filled from party lists. A party
qualifies for proportional representation by receiving

at least 5% of the nationwide party vote or winning an
electorate seat. List MPs are then added to electorate
MPs so that each qualifying party’s total representation
broadly matches its party-vote share.
An overhang arises when a party wins more electorate
seats than its party vote would otherwise entitle it to.
The party keeps those seats and Parliament expands,
which can raise the number required for a majority.
Government formation is separate from the allocation of

seats: a grouping must demonstrate that it can command
confidence in the House and pass supply. That can be
achieved through a formal coalition, a confidence-and-
supply agreement, or other support arrangements. The
largest party is not automatically guaranteed office and
minority governments are possible (e.g. although the
National Party secured more than 44% of the vote in the
2017 election, a minority Labour Party-NZ First coalition
was formed, supported by a confidence-and-supply

arrangement with the Green Party).
The overhang risk is unusually important in 2026 because
current polling leaves both blocs close to, but often
short of, a majority. In addition, the fragmentation of
the electorate increases the risk that one of the major
parties wins more electorates than implied by its party-
vote entitlement. This is in addition to the likelihood that
Te Pāti Māori will achieve an overhang given that it is
running an “electorate vote only” campaign and so asking

its supporters to cast their party votes for other left-
leaning parties. Each additional overhang seat expands
Parliament and so lifts the threshold needed to command
a majority.
The direction of the effect depends on which party
creates the overhang. Extra Te Pāti Māori electorate seats
would add MPs aligned more naturally with a Labour-
Green administration and could make Te Pāti Māori
support or abstention more consequential. A National
overhang would add seats on the right and could improve

the arithmetic for a National-ACT-NZ First government.
Under the latest RNZ-Reid Research projection, for
example, an overhang would arise if National won more
than 32 electorates or Te Pāti Māori won more than two.
This makes the party vote strategically important and
explains the focus on Opportunity’s threshold position.
A vote share just below 5% would produce no list seats
unless the party won an electorate (which based on
limited polling presently seems unlikely). A result just

above 5% would give it a parliamentary caucus large
enough, on current polling, to become the “kingmaker”
and decide which side can form a government. While
the Opportunity Party says that it could work with either
a centre-left or centre-right government, as detailed in
the appendix its broad policy platform might imply that it
more naturally aligns with the centre-left.
What the polls imply.
Kiwipoll’s six-poll tracker, last updated on 20 October,

put Labour on 27.9% and National on 28.7% - a gap
of just 0.8 percentage points. More strikingly, the two
major parties together attracted only 56.6%. The central
message across all polls is therefore fragmentation:
neither major party is close to governing alone, and almost
half of the represented vote is distributed among parties
that would shape the terms of any governing arrangement.
On that average, the balance between the established
blocs is also extremely narrow. The model associated

with the tracker assigned 56 seats to Labour, the
Greens and Te Pāti Māori, and 61 to National, ACT and
New Zealand First. Neither side reached a majority in a
presumed 124-seat parliament. Opportunity’s average
support of 6.2% translated into seven seats, enough to
take either bloc to the 63-seat threshold needed for a
majority. The average therefore implies that the identity
of the largest party may matter less than whether
Opportunity remains above 5% and which grouping it is

prepared to support.
This is not simply a close two-bloc contest. The polling
average also suggests that the minor parties would enter
negotiations with unusually large leverage. The Greens,
ACT and New Zealand First would consequently have
more scope than usual to influence tax, expenditure,
regulation, immigration and resource policy, or the
allocation of Cabinet positions, making it harder to
determine the likely form of a final coalition agreement.

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
That said, the leverage that these parties can apply on
their major party coalition partner is limited. The Green
Party cannot credibly threaten that it would form an
alternative coalition with the National Party. And neither
could ACT or NZ First credibly threaten to form an
alternative coalition with the Labour Party (while NZ First
has worked with the Labour Party in the past, it has ruled
out doing so on this occasion). Moreover, it is possible

that minor parties would be punished by the electorate
if a failure to form a government necessitated a second
general election, especially if the minor parties were
perceived to have made unreasonable demands or if the
electorate simply refocused on establishing a workable
parliamentary majority.
The Opportunity Party is the pivotal uncertainty because
its average position is above the threshold, but not
far above it. A result modestly below 5%, without an

electorate victory, would remove its votes from the
seat allocation and substantially change the arithmetic,
favouring the centre-right bloc. A result around its recent
6% polling average (or higher) would make the party
central to government formation.
Formally, National has ruled out working with
Opportunity, due to its objection to the latter’s tax policy.
For its part, Opportunity has said that it would talk first to
the party with the most seats, which on present polling

could be either Labour or National. However, as noted
previously, given Opportunity’s policy orientation, it
seems more likely that it would ultimately join a centre-
left coalition than a centre-right coalition.
To summarise, the bloc contest is close, and the route
to a majority depends on threshold and electorate
outcomes that remain uncertain. With that in mind, we
think that the following two scenarios are the most likely
outcomes of the election.

Scenario one: a returned centre-right
government, with greater minor party
representation.
The first scenario is that the existing coalition is returned,
albeit with the minor parties gaining strength and thus
additional leeway to pursue their policy agendas. We
assume that the coalition is not dependent on the
Opportunity Party to govern, which would be more likely
to occur if the latter fell marginally short of the party
vote 5% threshold needed to enter parliament in the

first place.
A renewed National-led government would begin with
considerable continuity. National’s fiscal plan retains
the current coalition’s focus on restoring surplus,
reducing debt as a share of GDP , keeping new spending
constrained and making greater use of planning reform
and private capital. It projects an OBEGALx surplus of
$4 billion in 2028/29, with net core Crown debt peaking
at 43.9% of GDP in 2027/28 and falling to 39.5% by
2030/31. National has ruled out new tax cuts during the

next term, saying tax relief should wait until the books
are back in surplus and surpluses are growing. But a
coalition in which ACT and New Zealand First held a
larger share of seats would be different from the current
more National-dominated administration. Unless its vote
share is particularly low, National would almost certainly
still provide the Prime Minister (sharing the role with NZ
First and ACT in the context of a three-year electoral
term seems quite unlikely – the latter will likely share the

Deputy role as they have in the current term). The three
parties share a common fiscal framework at the macro
level. However, National would have less ability to defer or
dilute its partners’ priorities.
Fiscal policy and the size of government.
ACT would press for a faster and deeper reduction in
the state’s footprint. Its fiscal plan proposes cutting
$9.5bn across the forecast horizon from spending outside
welfare and protected frontline services, reducing future

operating allowances by $400m a year to $2.0bn, and
moving non-welfare spending back towards 2017 real per-
capita levels. ACT says this would lower debt by $12.4bn
by 2031 relative to current forecasts. It also proposes
consolidating government into 19 departments and
18 ministers.
On its own, that agenda would pull a renewed
government beyond National’s more incremental
expenditure control. Coalition negotiations would likely
turn on the pace of implementation and the definition

of protected frontline services. The economic effect
would depend on whether savings represented genuine
efficiency gains or reductions in service capacity and
transfers. In the near term, a faster consolidation could
subtract from demand, while lower debt issuance
and a smaller structural deficit would strengthen the
fiscal buffer.
However, New Zealand First would push in a different
direction. Its programme combines selective tax relief
with active government intervention, regional grants

and strategic ownership. The tension within the coalition
would therefore not simply be between ‘more’ and ‘less’
government. ACT would seek a smaller and more market-
oriented state; New Zealand First would accept a larger
role where it supported national ownership, regions,
families or strategic industries. New Zealand First’s
proposal to reduce the corporate tax rate for SME’s to
20% (from 28%) may well be accepted by its coalition
partners during coalition negotiations – possibly phased

in over time – and would certainly be much cheaper
than the fiscal cost of some other policies promoted
by the party (notably compensating employees for
higher compulsory Kiwisaver contributions and the
nationalisation of the Bank of New Zealand).

### Page 4

4
Westpac New Zealand Economics
Tax, welfare and retirement.
ACT’s longer-run objective is a lower and flatter tax
system. Its immediate plan removes tax on qualifying
KiwiSaver investment earnings, ends the government
KiwiSaver contribution and returns net Emissions Trading
Scheme auction revenue to households through a
carbon-tax refund. Broader tax relief is deferred until
the books are balanced. ACT would raise the NZ Super
eligibility age by three months a year to 67 by 2035, while

allowing compassionate early access for people with long
careers in physically demanding work. It would also pause
contributions to the NZ Super Fund.
On welfare, ACT proposes tighter medical certification
and reassessment for disability-related benefits, a five-
year stand-down before migrants can access welfare,
and lower spending outside protected services. NZ
First would add its own eligibility restrictions, including
limiting NZ Super to citizens from 2029, while proposing

compulsory KiwiSaver enrolment from birth and a Crown
contribution. These policies would create difficult design
questions around transitional arrangements, hardship
and administration, but the broad direction would be
towards tighter eligibility and stronger conditionality.
Regulation, resources and labour markets.
A stronger ACT-NZ First presence would reinforce
deregulation in planning, employment and resource
development. ACT would seek property-rights-based

planning law, easier dismissal, a three-year minimum
wage freeze, a lower training wage for workers under
20 and removal of Te Mana o te Wai from consenting.
It would repeal the Zero Carbon Act. National’s own
RMA replacement and fast-track agenda would provide
substantial common ground.
NZ First would push more directly for regional and
strategic development: a special economic zone at
Marsden Point, offshore oil and gas surveying, expanded
mineral development, and the return of 50% of mining

royalties to producing regions. It also proposes breaking
up the power gentailers and has advocated bringing a
major bank back into New Zealand public ownership.
Both the National Party and NZ First have policies that
aim to force additional competition in the supermarket
sector, but these are opposed by the ACT Party.
Nonetheless, the result could be a government that is
simultaneously more deregulatory in consenting and
more interventionist in ownership and competition policy.

Immigration would also tighten. ACT proposes annually
reviewed work-visa categories, a Rural Workforce Visa,
stronger overstayer enforcement and physical-presence
requirements for permanent residents. NZ First proposes
replacing the Accredited Employer Work Visa with a
shortage-led Skilled Work Visa and holding a referendum
on a 20,000 annual cap on resident visas. National’s more
skills-focused approach would be under pressure from
both partners to place greater weight on enforcement,

settlement obligations and lower aggregate inflows.
Scenario two: Labour and the Greens,
supported by Opportunity.
The second main scenario that we view as likely is a
Labour Party-led coalition with the Green Party, with the
Opportunity Party supporting either inside the coalition
or via a confidence-and-supply arrangement. Depending
on precise outcome of the election, a confidence-and-
supply arrangement may also be required with Te Pāti
Māori to secure the necessary parliamentary majority.

A Labour-led alternative would represent a clearer break
with the current policy direction, but the size of that break
would depend on the success of the minor partners in
embedding their policies in the coalition agreement.
For its part, the Labour Party would aim to restore and
expand selected public services while remaining inside
its published fiscal framework. Its plan retains a $2.4
billion operating allowance for each Budget, targets an
OBEGAL surplus in 2028/29 and seeks to reduce net

debt below 20% of GDP over time. It would introduce
a 28% capital gains tax on post-1 July 2027 gains from
investment residential and commercial property, with
major exemptions, and use the revenue to help fund
health initiatives.
Labour’s central commitments include free prescriptions,
three free doctors’ visits, additional screening and
maternity services, 6,000 additional state houses,
public-transport fare caps, expanded apprenticeships, a
care-worker pay increase and partial reversal of planned

public-service cuts.
It would also restore the Reserve Bank’s dual price-
stability and maximum-sustainable-employment
mandate. As we have discussed in detail in a previous
note, in the current environment – dominated by supply
shocks – the return of the dual mandate could mean that
the RBNZ is prepared to accommodate a slower return of
inflation to target to reduce the impact on employment.
The Greens would shift the centre of gravity.
A strong representation of Green Party ministers in

the Cabinet would seek to move a coalition further on
redistribution, public ownership, housing and climate
policy. The party’s tax programme includes a 2.5% wealth
tax on net assets above $10 million excluding the family
home, a capital-acquisitions tax above $1 million with
exemptions, a 45% top personal rate, a higher company
tax rate for the largest firms, and a levy on large banks.
The stated objective is to raise about $5.1 billion net and
shift the tax burden from wages towards wealth and

large corporations.

### Page 5

5
Westpac New Zealand Economics
These taxes are needed to finance the Green Party’s
extensive promises on spending (see below). Formally,
the Labour Party has explicitly ruled out supporting the
Green Party’s tax policies to secure a Labour Party-led
coalition. Whether that pre-election commitment would
be reversed after the election is a matter of conjecture.
On spending and ownership, the Green Party proposes
free dental care and public transport, stronger income

support, expanded school lunches, more public housing,
a publicly owned renewable-energy entity and the
acquisition of supermarket stores to establish KiwiMart.
A centre-left government, especially with strong Green
Party representation, may find it harder to resist pressure
to lift public service salaries and the minimum wage
in response to fuel-driven inflation. Relative to PREFU
forecasts, this would imply additional expenditure that
would need to be met with either additional sources of

revenue or additional debt issuance.
Labour would resist parts of this agenda on cost,
implementation risk and fiscal credibility. The most likely
coalition bargain would therefore be a narrower set of
high-priority Green Party reforms, financed by some
combination of new revenue, reprioritisation and the
available Budget allowances.
The regulatory direction would be unambiguously
stronger. Labour and the Greens overlap on restoring
pay equity (which was not explicitly budgeted for in

Labour’s fiscal plan), strengthening worker protections,
improving rental standards, expanding public housing
and using government more actively in markets with
weak competition. Climate, freshwater and biodiversity
rules would tighten relative to the current government,
and mining on conservation land would face much
greater barriers.
Opportunity’s role, potentially outside Cabinet.
Opportunity could support a Labour-Green government,
either directly joining the coalition or through a confidence-

and-supply agreement. While the former would give
Opportunity seats at the Cabinet table, the latter
arrangement would let the party retain a distinct identity,
trading reliable support on confidence and budgets for a
defined policy programme. It may also provide more scope
for the party to “learn the ropes” with less public scrutiny,
given that none of its prospective MPs have ever been in
Parliament, let alone held Cabinet positions.
Regardless of what form the arrangement takes,

Opportunity’s landmark policies are neither a simple
extension of Labour nor of the Greens. Opportunity
proposes a “tax reset”, including a land value tax, with a
lower rate for rural land, alongside personal income tax
rates of 28%, 34% and 39%. The revenue raised would be
used to fund a “Citizen’s Income” payment to almost all
people, which would also replace several current social
welfare benefits. That said, Opportunity has conceded
that the land tax and Citizen’s Income are unlikely to be

implemented in the next Parliament given that it will
probably be the smallest of the coalition partners.
In negotiations, Opportunity would press for the
inclusion of its five bottom lines. These are (1) giving
the Commerce Commission more teeth and greater
independence to tackle excessive market power in
supermarkets and other uncompetitive sectors, (2)
regulating lobbying and capping political donations,
(3) developing a 10-year plan to "fix the health system"

alongside health professionals, with clear commitments
on funding, workforce and infrastructure, (4) phasing
out bottom trawling, working with industry, iwi and
coastal communities to fairly manage the transition,
(5) recommitting to Paris Agreement emissions
reduction obligations and protecting the Climate Change
Commission's independence. Opportunity’s other policies
include allowing net debt to exceed 50% of GDP if the
additional borrowing finances productive infrastructure.

It also proposes compulsory KiwiSaver contributions
rising to 6% for employees and employers (a policy it
shares with National), free public transport, stronger
competition enforcement, higher R&D spending and a
ten-year cross-party education plan. In a confidence
and supply arrangement Opportunity could reserve the
right to oppose other legislation it disagrees with, but
the arrangement would need sufficiently clear fiscal
guardrails to avoid repeated uncertainty over Budgets

and concerns about the stability of the Government.
The economic stakes.
The two scenarios imply meaningfully different policy
mixes, but neither government would operate without
constraints. The current fiscal forecasts already assume
a return to surplus and a declining debt ratio later in the
forecast period. Delivery depends on tight expenditure
control, revenue growth and an economic outlook
exposed to oil prices, global interest rates and domestic

productivity. Coalition agreements that materially add
to operating spending or reduce revenue would need
offsetting measures if the party heading the coalition
wished to preserve its stated fiscal track.
For monetary policy, the direct institutional contrast is
Labour’s proposal to restore the Reserve Bank’s dual
mandate. In practice, the larger near-term influence may
come through fiscal demand, labour market regulation,
migration and energy policy. A faster fiscal consolidation

on the right would tend to reduce demand pressure, while
a larger operating spending programme on the left would
increase demand pressure. Infrastructure investment
is somewhat different: if well selected and delivered, it
can lift productive capacity over the medium term, but
it can also add to near-term capacity pressure before
supply benefits arrive. And given the current interest rate
environment, the hurdle that must be crossed to justify
raising debt is higher than it has been for years.

$1bn Energy Sovereignty Fund for Māori and community renewable ownership; Māori-led environmental governance and
food sovereignty.
Regional
development
Devolve funding and decision-making to iwi and hapū; Māori-led housing, energy, agriculture and community infrastructure.

Minerals Emphasise rangatiratanga, environmental protection and Māori benefit and decision rights over natural resources.
Agriculture Māori food sovereignty, reject gene editing, and support Māori-led agriculture, land development and procurement.
Regulation Te Tiriti-based regulation, binding Tribunal recommendations, Māori authorities and equity procurement; stronger worker
and environmental protections.
Infrastructure Māori-led housing, renewable energy, agriculture and community infrastructure; greater iwi and hapū control over funding

and delivery.
Immigration Prioritise local training and Te Tiriti-based immigration settings.

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
Sticking with the plan
• The RBNZ left the OCR at 2.75% - a shock to
the market.
• The committee voted 4:3 against a 25bp
hike, with Governor Breman within the
“on hold” camp.
• The RBNZ delivered on its September strategy
of pausing to assess the impact of OCR
increases done to date.
• The MPC remains of the view that the OCR will
increase eventually, although views on the
timing differ among members.
• Westpac expects a 25bp hike in December and

continues to expect an eventual peak OCR of
4% in September next year.
• However, a December hike is not a done
deal and will depend on data on fuel prices,
inflation expectations and second-round
pricing pressures and information on evolving
global economic risks that accumulate in the
next six weeks.

Westpac forecast

Sources: Bloomberg, RBNZ, Westpac
Forecasts
OCR remains at 2.75%, further hikes remain
their base case.
Today the RBNZ left the OCR unchanged at 2.75%. This
was a shock to markets and most analysts as a 25bp hike
had been nearly fully priced by markets. The decision
went to a vote which resulted in a 4:3 split in favour of an
unchanged OCR.
The RBNZ has followed through on the pause it signalled
for this meeting back in the September Monetary Policy

Statement. All MPC members continue to see a case for a
higher OCR in time, but there are different views around
the timing of those increases. The Governor noted that
further interest rate increases remain likely in coming
meetings but are conditional on the whether the outlook
evolves in line with expectations.
The key case made by more hawkish MPC members
(Silk, Gourley and Conway) put greater weight on the
persistence of underlying inflation and the risk that higher

inflation becomes entrenched in price-setting behaviour.
They noted that core inflation has been above levels
consistent with the 2% target midpoint for some time,
28 Oct 2026 | Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
RBNZ October 2026 Monetary Policy Review.

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
and viewed aggregate economic growth as relatively
resilient despite weakness among many households and
businesses. In their view, tightening now would guard
against sustained inflation pressures and reduce the risk
of needing to do more later.
The case made by the majority in favour of no change
in the OCR (Breman, Hansen, Gai and Williams) argued
that the two recent OCR increases warranted more time
to assess their effects. They placed weight on inflation

having been weaker than expected in the Q3 data (the
Governor emphasised this point in answer to questions
at the press conference), smaller-than-anticipated
indirect effects from higher fuel prices, stable inflation
expectations and subdued wage growth. They also
emphasised downside risks to the recovery, particularly
the continued weakness in household consumption and
investment, and judged that there was little evidence
so far that businesses were using higher costs as an

opportunity to rebuild margins.
One point of note today is that the RBNZ seems
more concerned about risks to the global outlook,
notwithstanding its recent resilience – possibly
channelling some recent comments made by the IMF. The
RBNZ notes that geopolitical events, high energy prices,
and high and rising public and private debt levels pose
downside risk to global growth. It also notes uncertainty
about whether the surge in AI investment will be justified

by economic fundamentals. In addition, the MPC debated
whether higher global interest rates reflected a higher
neutral rate – due to higher inflation expectations and
demand for funding – or posed the risk of bringing
forward a potential global downturn (possibly leading to a
rapid decline in the event of an abrupt global adjustment).
The exchange rate has adjusted sharply lower today,
reflecting the market shock. The NZD/AUD exchange rate
has moved to a new cycle low of around 0.7980 at the

time of writing. This is in line with our year-end target for
the NZD/AUD exchange rate of 0.79 cents. Compounding
the downward pressure on the NZD/AUD exchange rate
has been strong CPI data in Australia combined with
hawkish RBA commentary. Our colleagues in Australia
continue to expect a Cash Rate hike in Australia on
Melbourne Cup Day and some risk of a further Cash
Rate rise in February. Hence downward pressure on the
NZD exchange rate seems a reasonable prospect given

interest rate differentials are weighing heavily on the NZD.
The RBNZ does not sound especially perturbed by the
prospect of weakness in the exchange rate and noted
that the low exchange rate is supportive of the medium-
term prospects of the export sector and the economy
more broadly.
Westpac outlook – December hike likely, but
not done deal.
We changed our forecasts from no change in the OCR
at the October meeting to a 25bp hike in the wake of the

CPI data last week. Given the absence of the hike today
we are reverting to the OCR forecast profile we had had
in place since July – i.e. one further 25bp hike in 2026
and then 25bp increases in the February and March
2027 meetings, followed by a slower pace of increases
at the June and September Monetary Policy Statement
meetings. Our forecast of a peak OCR of 4% from
September 2027 has remained unchanged throughout, as
has our assessment of a 3.75% neutral OCR.

That said, in the current uncertain environment, it goes
without saying that the evolution of monetary policy will
depend on how both global events and key data evolve
(we summarise the near-term economic diary below).
Therefore, as the RBNZ noted itself today, the exact timing
of the tightening profile is highly uncertain and even the
tightening we forecast at the December meeting should
not be regarded as a done deal.
Things to watch ahead of the 9 December

meeting.
The RBNZ’s next policy review is on 9 December, when it
will also publish a full Monetary Policy Statement (MPS)
and refreshed economic forecasts. How the RBNZ’s policy
stance evolves between now and then will depend on
both international and domestic factors.
Regarding the former, the MPC has clearly become
more concerned about risks to the global economic
outlook, stemming from geopolitical events, high energy
prices, high and rising public and private debt levels and

uncertainty about whether the surge in AI investment will
be justified by economic fundamentals. So, the RBNZ will
pay close attention to any developments that suggest
that downside risk to the global outlook is materialising.
There are only a few important domestic economic
indicators scheduled for release ahead of the 9 December
meeting (by contrast there is a full slate of indicators due
for release prior to the following meeting on 10 February).

We think the following are the key ones to watch.
• Q3 labour market surveys
(4 November): Indicators from these surveys will cast
light on economic activity in Q3, capacity pressures
in the labour market and whether the lift in headline
inflation is impacting growth in wages and salaries.
• Q4 RBNZ surveys
(17 November, 20 November, 24 November): Inflation
expectations measures from the RBNZ’s surveys of
professional forecasters, households and businesses

will be analysed to assess the impact of elevated
headline inflation.

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• October Selected Prices
(17 November): These pricing indicators will provide a
partial read on how inflation is tracking early in Q4.
• October/November ANZ Business Outlook
(29 October/30 November): The ANZ surveys will
provide a timely account of how business activity and
pricing behaviour is responding to the sharp rebound
in oil prices.
• October PMI and PSI surveys
(13 November/16 November): These may provide some

early insight regarding GDP growth in Q4.
In addition to the above, we will monitor a range of
other high-frequency indicators, such as monthly data
on filled jobs, consumer spending, building consents,
housing market activity and prices, job ads and consumer
confidence. We will also be paying close attention
to developments in prices for New Zealand’s key
export commodities.
Finally, with the General Election taking place on
7 November, it is possible that opinions regarding the

likely path of monetary policy could be impacted should
a Labour Party-led government – with an intention
to restore the RBNZ’s dual mandate – seem likely to
be formed.

SEPTEMBER CPI PREVIEW
Setting the pace for the run into year end
19 October 2026
Key points
• September CPI to rise 0.62%mth 
with the annual pace lifting to 4.1%yr 
from 4.0%yr. The seasonally adjusted 
CPI lifts 0.5%mth, a step down from 
the 0.7% increase in August.  
• September Trimmed Mean (TM) is 
set to lift 0.26%mth holding the 
annual pace steady at 3.6%yr. 
• As we noted in our review of the 
August CPI, our September quarter 
CPI estimate is unchanged at 1.3%qtr 
while the TM remained 0.9%qtr.  
• Auto fuel surged ahead of our 
expectations so along with signs of 
increase pass-through we lifted our 
December quarter CPI to 1.3%qtr/3.9%yr 
and the TM estimate to 0.9%qtr/3.5%yr. 
• This is a 0.8ppt increase to the CPI 
estimate, and a 0.2ppt increase to the 
TM estimate, highlighting inflation 
momentum meaningfully above 
the RBA’s current expectations.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
August inflation came in soft...
Inflation moderated in August following July’s strong 
outcome. The CPI rose 0.4% in the month, in line with our 
expectation but slightly below market consensus of 0.5%, 
taking the annual rate to 4.0%. On a seasonally adjusted 
basis, the CPI increased 0.7%mth, underscoring the impact of 
seasonal discounting in August and the start of the financial 
year repricing in July.
More closely watched are the measures of underlying inflation 
including the Trimmed Mean (TM) and market services. 
The TM rose 0.2% in August, in line with our expectations 
but again softer than the market, leaving the annual pace 
unchanged at 3.6% for a third consecutive month. Meanwhile, 
market services excluding volatiles & holiday travel increased 
0.2%mth/4.1%yr. Notable strength (three-month annualised, 
seasonally adjusted) was seen in sports participation (10.9%), 
clothing repair (7.0%), restaurant meals (6.8%) and other 
household services (5.2%). For full analysis of the August CPI, 
see “August CPI – Came in on our expectations ”.
The expenditure-weighted share of the CPI basket 
experiencing positive price shocks eased to 11% in August 
from 15% in July, while the share experiencing negative price 
shocks declined from 17% to just below 13%. The majority of 
the positive shocks were concentrated in consumer durables 
On a three-month moving average basis, the net momentum 
share (positive minus negative) rose to –4.5% from –7.7% 
in July and a trough of –15.8% in May, suggesting the 
disinflationary impulse may be nearing exhaustion. 
... with September due for a moderate pick-up
Looking ahead to September, early indicators painted a 
mixed picture. The NAB business survey showed retail price 
growth slowing to 0.9%qtr from 1.1%qtr in August, with 
labour cost growth easing to 1.7%qtr from 1.8%qtr, while 
purchase cost growth edged up to 2.4%qtr from 2.3%qtr. 
Nevertheless, all measures remain well above their long-run 
averages while the gap between purchase costs and final 
prices continues to widen, indicating businesses are facing 
margin compression (see here for further detail). Meanwhile, 
consumers’ year-ahead inflation expectations held steady 
at 4.9% in September (easing slightly at 2dp) but surged to 
5.3% in October likely reflecting higher fuel prices, with retail 
petrol and diesel averaging $2.20/l (+8.2%mth) and $2.68/l 
(+7.7%mth). We do believe the auto fuel prices are likely to see 
a near term peak in October.  Meanwhile, global shipping costs 
have also surged recently amid renewed Middle East tensions.
Sep quarter unrevised but Dec upgraded significantly
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Neha Sharma, Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The September CPI is expected to rise 0.6% (0.62% to 2dp)
with the annual pace lifting to 4.1%yr. Our seasonally adjusted
estimate increases 0.5% (0.48% to 2dp). For the monthly
TM, we expect a 0.3% (0.26% to 2dp) lift, holding the annual

pace steady at 3.6%. On a 3m/3m basis, the TM is tracking
a 1.0% gain in September (noting that while this measure is
conceptually different to the quarterly TM it still serves as a
useful guide). For market services excluding volatiles & holiday
travel we have pencilled in another 0.2% rise, slowing the
annual pace slightly to 4.0% (a nine-month low).
The largest contributors are recreation & culture (+0.27ppt),
driven wholly by holiday travel & accommodation. Transport

(+0.23ppt) makes a solid contribution, with gains in fuel
(+0.26ppt) partly offset by motor vehicles (–0.02ppt). Housing
also contributes (+0.13ppt), with rents and new dwellings
each adding 0.02ppt while property rates add 0.08ppt.
The key detractors in the month are food & non-alcoholic
beverages (–0.08ppt) and garments (–0.04ppt).
Key details for the month of September
Food & non-alcoholic beverages are expected to fall
0.5%mth, easing the annual pace to 2.8%yr. The weakness is

concentrated in a 3.7%mth decline in fruit & vegetables, while
non-alcoholic beverages are also expected to fall 1.5%mth.
Meals out & takeaway is forecast to rise 0.1%mth, easing the
annual rate to 3.9%.
Housing is expected to rise 0.6% in September, slowing the
annual pace to 5.5%yr. Component-wise:
• Rents are expected to rise 0.3%mth and 3.7%yr. CPI rent
inflation is nearing its peak, consistent with the moderation
in asking rent growth. We expect rent inflation to ease to

3.6%yr by end-2026 and 2.5%yr by end-2027.
• New dwelling prices, which capture only the building
component of construction costs, are expected to rise
0.2%mth and 4.9%yr. Ongoing weakness in established
housing markets would be increasing builders’ difficulty in
passing through higher costs to new builds. As a result, we
expect new dwelling inflation to moderate to 4.5%yr by
end-2026 and 2.1%yr by end-2027.
Transport inflation is expected to rise 2.0%mth in September,

lifting the annual pace to 7.7%yr reflecting an 8.3%mth
increase in fuel prices following higher oil prices through the
month. Motor vehicle prices and spare parts are expected to
fall 0.7%mth and 1.3%mth, respectively. In NSW, a one-year
vehicle registration discount between $80-100 came into
effect from 1 September, which is likely to weigh on the other
services in respect of motor vehicles component.
Recreation & culture is expected to rise 2.1%mth driven by a

4.4%mth increase in holiday travel, partly offset by a 0.4%
decline in audio, visual & computing equipment & services and
a 1.3% fall in sporting services.
